<header>The Second Wash.</header>

<meta>catchword: Your se-</meta>

Bulls <note>original: "Buls"; here meaning logical blunders or ridiculous contradictions</note>, not *poetically*, but *naturally*. I expected that you would oppose the *union of the light to the candle*, or that *of the soul to the body*, for it is the *point of controversy*; but you dwell upon circumstances which do not concern the union at all, and every one of which is false. Your first exception is that the light is *outside the candle, not in it*. It seems your reason is neither *within you* nor *outside of you*; for if it were *within you*, I suppose you would make use of it, and if it were anywhere *outside of you*, why is it not in your *book*? The light, my friend, turns the *tallow* into *vapor* with incessant heat, and the light *incorporates* into that vapor, so that the light is *in the candle*, not *outside of it*. But if by "candle" you mean that tallow which the light has not yet altered into a vapor, the light is indeed not *in that*, nor is there any reason it should be, for it is not yet united to it. Your se-

<summary>The author defends his metaphor of a candle's light to explain the union of soul and body, arguing that light is internal to the vaporized tallow rather than merely external. He accuses his opponent of failing to understand the basic mechanics of the physical process they are debating.</summary>
<keywords>soul and body, candle metaphor, Thomas Vaughan, natural philosophy, logic, tallow, vapor</keywords>

<page-num>169</page-num>
<header>The Second Wash.</header>

The second objection, *Mastix* <note>A shortened version of "Alazonomastix," the pseudonym of Vaughan's rival, Henry More</note>, is far more ridiculous than the former: "*The light* (you say) *is the effect of the candle.*" *Ha! ha! he!* Did you ever know a candle to <term>ignite</term> <note>original: tin; to kindle or light a fire</note> and generate a light from itself? If not, the light cannot be the *effect* of it. 

But tell me in all seriousness, does not the light come <term>from the outside</term> <note>original: *ab extra*</note>? Does not the candle borrow it elsewhere? I pray you, *Harry* <note>Henry More</note>, share this project of a *self-lighting* candle with the world: It will replace *touchwood* and *tinderboxes*, and silence the *clashing* <note>original: Chimes; here referring to the sound or sparks produced by striking flint</note> of *flint* and *steel*. 

But you have another <term>obvious blunder</term> <note>original: Bull rampant; a "bull" was a common term for a ridiculous logical contradiction or blunder</note>, and it is that which you call a third *argument*: "*The light of a candle* (you say) *is not always the same light, any more than water between the banks of a river is always the same water.*" Why so, *Mastix*? Does the light depart from the candle as the water does from the banks? I think not; for then you would be left in the dark, and truly

<summary>Vaughan mocks Henry More's argument that light is a physical "effect" generated by a candle, arguing instead that light must be introduced from an external source. He ridicules More's comparison of a candle's light to a flowing river, asserting that if the light actually "flowed away" like water, the room would be left in darkness.</summary>

<keywords>Thomas Vaughan, Henry More, Alazonomastix, light, candle metaphor, natural philosophy, soul and body, polemic, tinning, ab extra</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Thomas Vaughan’s polemical response to his critic, Henry More (writing under the pseudonym Alazonomastix). Vaughan is refuting More’s claims about the nature of light by explaining the physical process of combustion as an analogy for spiritual union.</meta>

<page-num>170</page-num>
<header>*The Second Wash.*</header>

you are in the dark already. Indeed, the body in which the light exists—namely, the vapor—is not always the same. For when the light has fed on the purer, oily <note>original: oleous</note> parts of it, the coarser <note>original: crude</note> portion, which is not suitable for the light to inhabit, escapes as smoke. But the light still provides itself with a new body as long as the candle lasts, and always remains one and the same light. 

Now I have broken your three blunt tools <note>Vaughan’s derogatory name for More’s three arguments</note>; but even if these foolish ideas <note>original: simplicities</note> had not been refuted, do you think they could have in any way disproved the union I spoke of? Get away from here, put your finger in your nose, and put something else in your mouth; for you would do well to stop speaking until you are able to talk sense. You have indeed so disgraced your Mother University <note>Cambridge, where Henry More was a fellow</note> that the students should drag you to the bridge and tumble you like a rag-doll <term>Lent of Clouts</term> <note>original: Lent of Clouts; a straw-filled puppet or "Jack-o'-Lent" used as a target for abuse or thrown into water during the season of Lent</note> into the River Cam <note>original: Cham</note> to prevent further dishonor to them.

<margin>Observation</margin>

<summary>Vaughan explains that while the fuel of a candle changes as it burns into smoke, the light itself remains a constant entity. He concludes by mocking More's logic and suggesting he has disgraced Cambridge University.</summary>

<header>The Second Wash.</header>

## Observation 9.

"All that I say there is," etc. Here you say—and with you a certain *Theu-polus* <note>A pseudonym used by Henry More, likely "Theopolopolus"</note>—that the "sense of the soul" (for that is what *Virgil* <note>The Roman poet Virgil, specifically referring to Book VI of the *Aeneid*, which contains a famous passage about the "Spirit within" and the "Mind" that moves the world</note> mentions) is the clothing <note>original: vestiment</note> of the soul; but I say this is nonsense. It is true that the Soul itself <note>original: Anima</note> is the vehicle of the Mind <note>original: vehiculum Mentis; in Neoplatonic philosophy, the 'Mind' is the highest, divine portion of the human spirit, which rides within the 'Soul'</note>, the vehicle of the higher *intellectual portion*. However, you do not know what spirit it is that serves as the vehicle of the soul <note>original: vehiculum anima</note>—the vehicle or clothing of the *sensual soul* <note>the part of the soul responsible for physical senses and emotions</note>—about which the Poet speaks. Therefore, be silent, you *quacks*! Or speak only of what you understand.

Now Master Mastix <note>Vaughan’s nickname for his opponent, from More’s pseudonym Alazonomastix</note>, having lost yourself in an inferior topic <note>original: Theme</note>, you have the nerve <note>original: impudence</note> to attempt a more *majestic* one. You run to my *intelligent light* like a fly to a candle, but your folly has cost you your wings; you are burned for your boldness <note>original: saucinesse</note>, and

<summary>Vaughan argues that Henry More misunderstands the nature of the soul and its "vehicles" in the poetry of Virgil. He distinguishes between the higher intellectual mind and the lower sensual soul, mocking More's lack of expertise in spiritual philosophy.</summary>

<keywords>Thomas Vaughan, Henry More, Virgil, Anima, Mind, Sensual soul, Intelligent light, Neoplatonism, polemic</keywords>

<page-num>172</page-num>
<header>The Second Wash.</header>

now you stink like the *snuff of a candle*. In the first place, Master *Maurus* <note>original: Maurus; a derogatory nickname for Henry More</note>, you lay a foundation for your future nonsense and tell me what *fire* is. It is (you say) *a fluid body, swiftly and variously agitated.* So is the *air* too, *Mastix* <note>Vaughan’s nickname for More, from More’s pseudonym Alazonomastix</note>, and the *wind*. Since your definition is so broad, it would not be out of place to ask you: to which of these three do you apply it? But you are a skillful *logician*; the *essential difference* <note>a technical term in logic for the specific quality that distinguishes one species from another within a genus</note> is still to come. Now, readers, take note of this:

*The fire is a body made of particles which do not rest next to one another, but rub* <note>original: fridge; an archaic term meaning to rub, chafe, or move restlessly</note> *against one another.* 

Ha! Ha! He! Surely your *tailor’s goose* <note>a heavy iron used by tailors, named for its handle which resembled a goose's neck; it would "hiss" when hot</note> was heated with this *fire* when it *hissed* at you, as if identifying another *goose* for an acquaintance. 

But let us examine this *essential difference*, if it truly is one. *Fire (you say) consists of particles.* But do not the *air* and the *water* also consist of particles? Yes, truly, *Mastix*, according to your *philosophy*, and

<summary>Thomas Vaughan mocks Henry More’s definition of fire as a fluid body of agitated particles, arguing that such a vague description fails to distinguish fire from air or water.</summary>

# The Second Wash.

So, you have given us a *definition* of *fire* that also applies to *water* and *air*, for there is no *distinguishing difference* in it. But come now, you *drudge* <note>original: Hackney; a horse for hire, or a person who does dull, routine work for another</note> of *Descartes*! I tell you, I have already refuted your *particles* and clearly demonstrated their *emptiness* <note>original: vanitie</note>. Let me know then: for what purpose do you mention *fire* in this place? I will tell you, *Harry* <note>Henry More</note>, and so answer for you. 

You have *created* a soul made of *ordinary kitchen-fire* and *atoms*, and then you spent *five pages* refuting your own creation. The soul, *Mastix* <note>Vaughan’s nickname for More, meaning "scourge"</note>, which I spoke of, is a *living, intelligent Fire* or *Light*—a *Light* which descends from the *first Father of Lights* <note>A reference to God; see James 1:17</note>, and a *Fire* which proceeds from *God himself*, who is a *consuming fire* <note>A reference to God; see Hebrews 12:29</note>. 

Now, my friend, I will see what you have to say against *this Fire*; and before I am finished, that very *Fire* shall answer everything you say. Prepare yourself then, *Harry*, and as I have told you elsewhere,

prick up your *Puritan* ears:
<margin>Hear the voice of the fire <note>original: Κλῦθι πυρὸς / Audi Ignis vocem</note></margin>

Your attempts against this *Fire-Mastix* <note>original: Fire-Mastix; likely a reference to Vaughan himself or his work as a "scourge" (mastix) against More’s errors regarding fire</note> begin with a demand, and it is this: *Is there (you say) any substance in this fire which we may call its essential form <note>original: Essential forme; in Aristotelian philosophy, the specific quality that makes a thing what it is</note>, or not?* Your question, my friend, is a *blunder* <note>original: Bull; a ridiculous or self-contradictory statement</note>, for I told you the *very soul was a fire*. Now you ask me if this *fire* has an *essential form*, which is the same as asking me if the soul has a soul! 

But so that I may steady and fix your dizzy *brains*, I will give you a quick and full answer. *This fire is truly a fire, but a divine, living one. It is, at the same time, the essential, intelligent form itself, and in all of this, there is no inconsistency.* "Yes, there is," says *Henry More*: "*For how can it organize the body, when the parts of this fire tend as much this way as that way—or at least tend only one way, for instance, upward?*" You are indeed a

# The Second Wash.

...rare disputant, but I would have you know that these *motions* belong to a *kitchen fire*, out of which stuff you have made your *soul*. But the *fire* I speak of is a *living, intelligent fire*, and its motions are neither casual nor necessary <note>Meaning they are not random nor forced by physical laws</note>, but they are voluntary; for it acts as it wills, expands and contracts itself, and moves and rests as it thinks fit.

This is the true active nature, *Mastix* <note>Vaughan’s nickname for Henry More, meaning "scourge"</note>, and this is the *principle of motion and rest* <note>original: Principium Motus & Quietis; an Aristotelian definition of the essence of nature</note>. Your second argument is a ridiculous, fanatic *folly*, and it runs like this:

> *How can this fire give form to the whole body of a man? For it was only large enough when he was a child; it will be too small for him now that he is a man, unless we suppose it grows and is nourished?*

Now, sir, I must ask you: did the tailor, whose *iron goose* <note>original: Iron-goose; a heavy pressing iron used by tailors, named for its handle which resembled a goose's neck</note> you mentioned earlier, ever take measure of your *soul*, so that you should know how big or how small it is? # The Second Wash.

...too small for a man just because it is large enough for a child? But in all seriousness, Sir *Maurus* <note>original: Sir Maurus; Vaughan's mocking name for Henry More</note>, what scope will you allow to the soul’s *sphere of activity*? Will you allow it no more than what *Hans* occupies—*the little Boy in the Basket* <note>likely a reference to a puppet or a character in a popular performance of the time</note>?

Leave the child alone, *Harry*. For even if he should grow as tall as *Og of Bashan* <note>a biblical giant and King of Bashan mentioned in the Book of Deuteronomy</note>, his soul will have enough fire and power to *animate* <note>original: informe; in the philosophical sense of giving life and form to physical matter</note> him. The rest of your arguments only destroy a soul that you yourself have invented: namely, a soul made of particles or *atoms*. 

However, I cannot help but notice that you mention, in passing, that *fire* is devoid of sense and therefore cannot be a soul. May the readers thank you for that *information*! Once again, you show them how you struggle against *kitchen fire* and the *atoms* of *Descartes* <note>original: Des Chartes; the French philosopher René Descartes</note>, whom you have mistaken in this note for *Eugenius Philalethes* <note>Thomas Vaughan’s pseudonym</note>. From this point until the end of your *chapter*, you tell us about an *atom* in the middle,

# The Second Wash.

...and an *atom* at the *outside*, with *rectilinear* <note>moving in a straight line</note> and *circular motions* of *particles*. Then you fall upon *lines*, *centers*, *square figures*, *parallels*, *perpendiculars*, *points*, and *orbs*: and all this to make your *fry* <note>original: frie; a swarm or small group, often referring to young fish</note> of *atoms dance*. Lastly, you bring your *soul* out of the *body* to *conflict* with the boisterous winds, and here your *kitchen fire* dies, and *Lucretius* <note>Titus Lucretius Carus, a Roman poet and philosopher who argued that the soul was made of physical atoms and perished with the body</note> writes the *epitaph*. Alas, poor *soul*! It was *blown out* by the winds as other *flames* and *candles* are, for it was made of the *same stuff*; and now *Mastix* <note>original: Mastix; part of More's pseudonym, Alazonomastix, "the scourge of boasters"</note> the *burnt wick* <note>original: snuffe</note> of it *smokes*, and your *observation* stinks at its *conclusion* <note>original: cloze</note>. Truly Sir, if I were disposed to employ my *wit*, I have a fair opportunity to *make sport*: no man can desire more advantages than you have given me in this *chase* after your own *chimeras* <note>imaginary monsters or wild, unrealistic fantasies</note>. But at this time it is not my *pleasure* <note>original: placet</note>: I will for once show you *mercy*, but with this *sober judgment* <note>original: Censure</note>, that your *philosophy* is but

Thus, Sir, I have forgiven your many *sophistries* <note>clever but fallacious arguments intended to deceive</note>, but a single *bull* <note>a logical contradiction or a ludicrous blunder in speech</note> I cannot overlook. It is a fault I have a particular grudge against, especially when it comes from a *Master of Arts* and a *teacher* <note>original: pedagog</note> at *Christ’s College* [Cambridge]. *“The Soul,” (you say) “is a spiritual substance without physical dimensions, yet it has an immaterial vastness that can be expanded and contracted.”* Is this not a *Bull Mastix* <note>a pun combining a "bull" (blunder) with More's pseudonym "Mastix," likening him to a bull-mastiff dog</note>, or shall I call it a *problem*? That shall be our new name for a *bull* between you and me, so that I do not expose you too often. Why, *Harry*! A *substance* without *dimensions*, and yet *expandable* and *contractible*? What is this? A thing that is *long* without *length*, and though it has no *length*, it may be *shortened*? I ask you: does the *soul* have the same boundaries or limits when she is expanded as when she is contracted? If

## Observation 10.

You are indeed very good at *analogies* <note>original: Similitudes</note>, etc. You will find me to be the same before I have finished with you, but I will not rant and bluster <note>original: rayle and vapour</note>; it is no part of my business. Here you are fooling with my *Magical Chain* by which the *Soul* descends to *physical birth* <note>original: Generation; referring to the Neoplatonic idea of the soul descending from the heavens into a material body</note>, and you would gladly unravel it. Let us see then how you set to your work, for you speak of a bare point, which

will prove but a blunt one... You say *that three portions of Light should pull up two, or five should pull up one, rather than one should pull down three, or five, or two.* Is not this your *bare point*? It is indeed bare of all understanding, for you do not consider what you say: Do *Spirits* move upwards or downwards when they apply themselves to *birth* <note>original: Generation; in this context, the process of a spirit entering the material world or a body</note>? Which way do they incline, *Harry*, toward the body, or away from the body? If toward the body, why should the *Soul* attract upwards contrary to her own inclination? You are indeed a total *ignoramus*! Do you not know that every *Agent* <note>original: Agent; an active force or being that produces an effect</note> acts according to the laws of *universal Nature*, and the end prescribed by those *Laws*? Now the goal here is *birth*, and the inferior *Light* prepares <note>original: qualifies</note> the *Body* and makes it capable of receiving the *Superior* [Light]; for *like attracts like* <note>original Latin: simile simili gaudet</note>, and *Higher things* will easily agree with *Lower things* when they are tempered and made similar to them in *constitution* <note>original: Complexion; the specific balance of qualities or "humors" making up a substance</note>.

<page-num>181</page-num>

<header>The Second Wash.</header>

<sig>N3</sig>

This preparation makes the *Soul* descend naturally, as to a place fitted for *her*, but that she should attract her *Body* or any part of it upwards is a thing contrary to her own propensity <note>original: propension; a natural tendency or inclination</note>, and destructive to the *Generative Laws* of *Nature*. Nay, it is destructive to the very *Nature* of the *Soul* that she should work contrary to her own *Inclinations* and *Motions*: and what is more absurd, it is contrary to that end for which the very *Magnetism*, or *symbol of Principles* <note>In alchemy and Hermetic philosophy, this refers to the shared "signatures" or spiritual affinities that allow higher spirits to connect with lower matter</note> was *Ordained*. You may as well return to your *kitchen-fire* and tell me it is the *Nature* of *fire* to ascend, and therefore the *Sunbeams* and *Light of Heaven* should not descend. You are, my friend, a stranger in the *School of Nature*; you do not know the mutual *Conspiracy* <note>original: Conspiracie; from the Latin "conspirare," meaning "to breathe together" or act in perfect harmony</note> and that miraculous *League* <note>An alliance or bond</note> which exists between *superiors* <note>Higher, spiritual, or celestial entities</note> and *inferiors* <note>Lower, material, or earthly entities</note>.

<header>The Second Wash.</header>

## Observation 13.

"Assure yourself, Eugenius," etc. I do assure myself, Mastix <note>A pseudonym for Henry More, the philosopher Vaughan is debating; "Mastix" is Greek for "whip" or "scourge"</note>, that you cannot *answer* even *one* of them. Can you tell me *why grass is green?* If you cannot even tell me *this*, how do you dare pretend to have the ability to resolve those questions which I proposed to you concerning the *Seminal Reasons* <note>original: Rationes Seminales; in Stoic and Neoplatonic philosophy, these are the formative, spiritual principles or "blueprints" within nature that guide the growth of all living things</note>, which you have never *seen*? Nor have you seen their *operations* in their general *spermatic chaos* <note>original: spermaticall Chaos; in alchemy, the primal, unformed matter that contains the "seeds" or potential for all life</note>? Truly, Harry <note>Henry More</note>, you are so far from *knowing* them that I dare say you cannot even translate *Rationes Seminales* into English and give me the true meaning of the concept. You have indeed taken a clever course to save your reputation: your answer is that *you think it fit to answer none of them.* Ha! Ha, he! But I will now see what your answer is to those arguments which I urged to prove the *Central Artist* <note>original: Centrall Artist; the divine or spiritual intelligence acting within the center of matter to organize and shape it</note>,

If the *Central Artist* does not know what he does, then he has no knowledge at all; and this indeed you do grant, for (you say) he has no *Reason* or *conscious awareness* <note>original: animadversion; the act of noticing or perceiving something</note> in himself. Tell me then: is the *Central spirit* of man a *Knowing spirit* or not? If he is (and without question he is), why should he not know what he does? If he is not a *Knowing spirit*, how does man then come to be a *Knowing creature*?

# The Second Wash.

Why so, *Mastix*? <note>original: "Mastix"; short for "Alazonomastix," the pseudonym used by Henry More, meaning "scourge of braggarts"</note> Because the *stars* are nearer to the *sun* than this *earth* is to the *stars*? Ha! ha! he! But you have another argument to *divorce* the *sun* and the *stars*. You say the lively vibration of their *light* shows plainly that it is their own, not borrowed. It shows indeed that they are at a great distance from us, for the *scintillation* <term>the twinkling of a star</term> of the *fixed stars* is, by all *philosophers* but yourself, attributed to their *vast distance* <note>original: "vast Removall"</note>. But as to the nature of their *light*, it proves nothing. This is all, Master *More*, that you have objected to me and my *philosophy*. The rest of your book is a supposed *victorious rant*. You are full of a tumultuous pride, and for want of another person to proclaim your exploits, you cry out yourself, "*Look, there lies the contagious ghost of Ephesus!*" <note>original: "Loe there lyes the contagious spectrum of Ephesus!" Therefore (*he says*), with my feet standing lightly on the shoulders of all the sects of the Earth, and with my head stooping down out of the clouds, I will venture to test the world with this serious question: Tell me, O all you nations, peoples, and kindreds of the Earth, what is the reason that the world is such a stage of misery for the sons of men? Is it not because that which should be their primary guidance—their religion and the highest light of their minds—is nothing but heat and squabbling over subtle, uncertain points and a foolish affectation of high mysteries? Meanwhile, the undisputed, sober truths of virtue and piety are neglected, and the simplicity of the life of God is despised as a most contemptible thing.

And I had no sooner spoken these words in my mind, but it seemed to me I heard an answer from every direction

*Perhaps you will tell me that this is just a rattle of angelic figures of speech* <note>original: "Seraphicall Tropes" - high-flown or celestial metaphors.</note> *and a mystery of metaphors. <meta>This page continues Thomas Vaughan’s critique of Henry More. On the previous page, Vaughan quoted More’s claim that his own verses are as eternal as the word of God, ending with the biblical phrasing "Heaven and earth may pass away, but not one tiny bit of this truth shall..."</meta>

<header>To the Reader.</header>

...pass away. What horrible, intolerable blasphemy is this? Our Savior is not speaking in this text about the "dead letter"—which is nothing but ink and paper <note>original: "papyr"</note>—for without doubt, that physical word cannot outlast heaven and earth, unless we imagine <note>original: "fansie"</note> a library in the New Jerusalem and a translation of some Geneva Bibles <note>A popular 16th-century English translation of the Bible, often associated with Puritanism.</note> into those eternal archives. 

He speaks here of the substantial, spiritual word, which he mentions elsewhere explicitly: "Man does not live by bread alone, but by every word that proceeds out of the mouth of God" <note>Matthew 4:4</note>. It is a wretched impiety that this barbaric scribbler should drool out four rotten verses and then tell us they are coeternal <note>Existing eternally alongside</note> with the word of God. 

I have cited these passages, Reader, to give you a clear impression <note>original: "Expreſſe"</note> of the man and his character; now, I leave it to your judgment whether he does not deserve a sharp reproof. Perhaps a corrosive <note>A harsh or biting substance; here, a stinging critique intended to "eat away" at More's arrogance.</note> well applied might make him aware of his blasphemies.

## Upon the Publication <note>original: "Edition"</note> of This Book

How is the wisdom of the sages gone,
Chained in the state of dark oblivion?
Is there more among the failing mortals now
With a revolution <note>original: "Revolution"; likely referring to a cyclical change or a great turning point in knowledge.</note> like yours?
Faith! Mark her! If you can perceive
That your coat had need of such a sleeve! <note>An idiomatic expression suggesting that this addition or distinction completes or improves the whole.</note>
It is a distinction, and it continues
As if he would detect your own.
Ennoble the characters within,
And wear by right the title of Sovereign King.

# To the most Excellent
## Philosopher Eugenius
### *Philalethes,*
## Upon the frenzies of Henry More <note>original: "Harry Moore"; Henry More (1614–1687) was a Cambridge Platonist philosopher and a fierce intellectual rival of Thomas Vaughan.</note>,
### the Ballad-monger <term>ballad-monger: a derogatory term for an inferior poet or a writer of cheap, popular verses</term>.

How! Is the madman <term>original: "Bedlam"; referring to a patient of Bethlehem Hospital (Bedlam), London's notorious insane asylum</term> loose? And to be seen
Chained in the stanzas of Edmund <note>original: "Mun"</note> Spenser's *Queen* <note>Referring to Edmund Spenser’s *The Faerie Queene*. Henry More wrote his philosophical poetry, such as *Psychozoia*, in Spenserian stanzas, which the author is here mocking.</note>?
Is More among the fairies? Does he come now
With revelations from the lunar bow <note>The "lunar bow" (a moon-rainbow) suggests More’s ideas are as ethereal, fleeting, and "moony" as a rainbow at night.</note>?
Faith! Queen Mab <note>The legendary Queen of the Fairies.</note>, look to it: If you admit this mouse,
Your court will need to become a house of correction <term>house of correction: a place of punishment and reform for vagrants or the mentally ill</term>;
He is a distracted vermin, and runs on
As if he would depose your Oberon <note>The King of the Fairies.</note>,
Monopolize <note>original: "Engrosse"</note> the Crystal Castle for himself
And wear by right the title of Sovereign Elf.

<meta>catchword: Poor</meta>

--- Page 44 ---
<meta>This page continues a satirical poem addressed to Eugenius Philalethes (Thomas Vaughan), mocking his rival Henry More. The previous page introduced More as a "madman" wandering through the verses of Spenserian poetry.</meta>

Your progress is poor! An orchard and a dairy
Were the well-known haunts of the most *wandering fairy,* <note>original: "Errant fairie"; "Errant" means wandering, but also punningly refers to a "knight-errant," mocking More's use of chivalric Spenserian language.</note>
But *More* commands three worlds; he is brother to
The *Sun* and *Moon*; he has the *Moguls* <note>The Mughal Emperors of India, symbols of vast wealth and power.</note> below
As his *tributaries*, and is considered the man
Whose style surpasses the sons of *Ottoman.* <note>The Ottoman Sultans.</note>
He, like those giants who built in *Shinar,* <note>The biblical land where the Tower of Babel was built by those attempting to reach heaven through human pride.</note>
Can scale the skies, and from the center tilt
Himself into the clouds, where he will lie
As *Ixion* <note>In Greek mythology, Ixion was a king who tried to seduce the goddess Hera but was tricked by Zeus into embracing a cloud-phantom.</note> did, a *sentry in a hidden post* <term>perdue: a soldier placed in a dangerous or exposed position; a hidden scout</term> and a *spy;*
So quick and secret too, that in this retreat <term>repair: a place one frequents, or the act of going to a place</term>
He (like the devil) seems to be the *Prince of the Air.* <note>A traditional biblical title for Satan (Ephesians 2:2), used here to suggest More's arrogance or spiritual delusion.</note>
Nor does this frolic end in a cloud, for he
(To show more tricks to dull humanity)
Descends again, and through those cloudy portals
Steals (like the nightmare) to ride weary mortals;
He treads their shoulders, asks questions, and answers too,
And with the world’s four *quarters* has business,
Both *East* and *West*; the farthest *North* and *South*
Make *joint replies* to his *almighty mouth;*
With *acclamations,* too, never used for men—
Such as *Hallelujah, It is so, Amen!*
And yet he’s no *enthusiast* <term>enthusiast: in the 17th century, a derogatory term for someone claiming direct, private divine inspiration or religious ecstasy</term>, nor allied
To *Independency* <note>A radical religious movement that favored the autonomy of individual local churches.</note>, but *Scotified,* <term>Scotified: influenced by or adhering to Scottish Presbyterianism, which was often viewed with suspicion by English occultists and Royalists</term>
For though he makes some *journeys* to the skies,
Yet he was never caught up to *Paradise.* <note>A jab implying that for all his "celestial" philosophy, More lacks true divine insight or salvation.</note>

<summary>In this portion of the satirical poem, the author mocks Henry More’s perceived intellectual and spiritual arrogance, comparing his "heavenly" philosophical travels to the Tower of Babel and the myths of Ixion. The poet portrays More as a delusional "Prince of the Air" who claims divine authority but is actually a religious pretender.</summary>

--- Page 45 ---
<meta>This page continues a satirical attack on the philosopher Henry More. The previous page mocked More’s spiritual arrogance, comparing him to the "Prince of the Air" and a "nightmare" riding the shoulders of mortals.</meta>

He is surely no *Saint*, but a zealous *More* <note>original: "Moore"; a pun on the author Henry More's name and "Moor," which was often used at the time to imply someone dark, heathen, or exotic.</note>
Whose conscience calls his *Mother-Church* a whore.
Though in all *seriousness* <note>original: "sadness"</note> he has wondrous *quirks*, <term>firks: sudden freaks, tricks, or movements of the mind</term>
And may well confuse the *Scottish* and *English Churches* <note>original: "Kirks"</note>.
Why, *Knipperdoling* <note>Bernhard Knipperdoling (c. 1495–1536) was a leader of the radical Anabaptist rebellion in Münster, known for religious extremism and bizarre behavior.</note>, search him over and over,
Never imagined grosser lies, nor uttered more.

You *flying Goose*, cousin to *Domingo’s* train <note>A reference to Domingo Gonsales, the protagonist of Francis Godwin’s *The Man in the Moone* (1638), who traveled to the moon using a team of wild swans called "gansas."</note>,
A *metaphysical Gansa*, whose vulgar brain
Tells more lies than old *Lucian* <note>A satirist of the 2nd century AD, author of *A True Story*, a famous collection of tall tales about space travel.</note>, and like some puffed-up *old man* <term>Gaffer: an elderly man, often used here to imply a rustic or foolish person</term>
Tells tales more monstrous than *Leo the African* <note>original: "Leo Afer"; Leo Africanus was a 16th-century traveler whose accounts of Africa were sometimes viewed as exaggerated by Europeans.</note> does;
Do you think the *world* (when they see these whims)
Will admire anything in you but a mad head?
Or that your *Pen* <note>original: "Quill"</note> shall make you a *Platonist*
Because that *Star* you imagine is actually a *Mist*?
Base, shameless fool! who mistakes the *Moon’s* dull *Ray*
For the *active day* and *Expanded Light*,
And like some *bird of night*, because your *Eyes* fail,
You adore *Shadows* and complain at *Sunbeams*.

But tell me, will *Erra Pater* <note>A pseudonym for a famous astrologer or almanac-maker, used here to represent a source of traditional wisdom or prophecy.</note> submit
To all the *decrees* <note>original: "Placets"</note> of your *Sodomite*?
Are you his *hired submissive* <term>Hackney-Pathick: a "hackney" is a horse for hire; a "pathic" is a passive partner in a same-sex relationship. The author is using heavy sexual insults to mock the philosophical intimacy between More and his followers.</term>? Will you allow
The *lusts* of his *foul wit*, and *body* too?
Or must no one understand what *Plato* did
Except *More*, and *Aster*, his *white Ganymede* <note>In ancient sources, Aster was a youth loved by the philosopher Plato. Ganymede was the beautiful youth abducted by Zeus to be his cupbearer; both references are used here to imply an inappropriate or homoerotic intellectual relationship.</note>?
Must that delicious *spirit* be confined
To your *Mind*, just as it is to his *Body*?

<meta>This page continues a satirical attack on the philosopher Henry More. The previous page compared him to religious extremists and legendary liars like Lucian, mocking his claim to have a unique understanding of Platonism.</meta>

Speak, man, are you *unique* <note>original: "singular"</note>? Or are you *mad*?
Must we, to make you good, all be judged *bad*?
Must *white* and *red* sue for approval
Because the *recently discovered world* is *black* and *blue*?
Please, declare! Let’s have your *reasoning in writing*,
That future readers may owe you their *wisdom*,
And say that you—like the *Saints*—claim that before you
No one could ever see into *Plato’s* mind.
Had the serious reader of the *Faerie Queene*—
The *Squire*, your *father*, seen this *madness* of yours,
(Who, while his *Eve* <note>Refers to his wife, Henry More’s mother.</note> held the *candle*, read
*Spenser’s* provocative poetry, and then went to *bed*)
He would have said, and sworn it by *himself*,
That those *winter lectures* had produced an *elf* <note>A reference to the "Elphin" knights of Spenser’s poem, suggesting Henry is a creature of fantasy rather than a rational man.</note>.

Over your *Negro’s* looks <note>A pun on the author's surname, More, equating him with a "Moor" or "Negro."</note> a borrowed *fine linen veil* <term>lawn: a fine, sheer cloth often used for the sleeves of bishops</term>,
You have clothed the scabby sheep in the *lamb’s fleece*,
Making your *junior patron* <note>original: "Punie patron"; "puny" could mean small, but also refers to "puisne," a junior judge or associate.</note> the sponsor of the *piece*;
Surely I commend you, it was a *prudent move*
To trust your *pupil* only with your *wares*;
You dared not appoint a *strict tutor*
To be the judge of your *gross, Moorish brain*,
For he (no doubt) would have whipped you for being *absurd*,
And added a *third lash* to your *second*.
And you, his *rhyming fool*, half of whom lurks

You crutch in meter, a mere trick to beg,
Like a "God bless your worship" on a wooden leg,
Pull down your rag <note>original: "Clout"</note>; the age has grown too wary
To be caught by a sign saying, "Here is a good tavern" <term>Ordinary: a tavern or eating house where a fixed-price meal was served</term>.

Shine above envy and the common arts,

A spark of the first fire! Whose eagle-flights
Do not trade with Earth and coarseness, but pass
To the pure heavens, and make your God your mirror <note>original: "glasse"</note>,
In whom you see all forms, and so you give
These rare discoveries of how things move and live;
Proceed to make your great design complete,
And do not let this loathed Moor <note>original: "Moore"; a pun on the surname of Henry More, the author's rival</note> defeat our hopes;
Shake off the eclipse, this dark *intruding veil*
Which would force night upon us, and *bequeath* <term>entail: to settle property or consequences on descendants</term>

Has lost himself—to our descendants.
Down with all you stale impostures! Castles built
In the air, and guarded by your reverend beard,
Brat of *Nicomachus* <note>Nicomachus was the father of Aristotle; the author is dismissing Aristotelian philosophy as an "imposture."</note>! I will no longer

Your tyrant-text; but by this dawning light
Which streams upon me through your dense <term>three-piled: literally referring to the thickest, highest-quality velvet; here meaning extremely thick or deep</term> night,
I shall pass to the East of truth, until I may see
Man’s first, fair state; when wise simplicity,

A spark of the first fire! whose *eagle-flights*
Do not trade with the earth and its coarseness, but pass
To the pure heavens, and make your God your mirror <note>original: "glasse"</note>,
In whom you see all forms, and so provide
These *rare discoveries* of how things move and live:
Proceed to make your great design complete,
And do not let this loathed *Moore* <note>original: "Moore"; a punning reference to the philosopher Henry More</note> defeat our hopes.
Shake off the *eclipse*, this dark, *intruding veil*
Which would force night upon us, and *entail* <note>original: "Intayle"; to settle a legacy or property on future generations</note>
The same gross *ignorance*—in whose shadows he
Has lost himself—upon our posterity.
Down with all you stale *impostures*! Castles built
In the air, and guarded by your "reverend beard,"
You *brat of Nicomachus* <term>Brat of Nichomachus: a derogatory reference to the Aristotelian tradition, as Nicomachus was the son of Aristotle and the namesake of his "Ethics"</term>! I will no longer
Bow to your ancient handful <note>original: "hoarie handfull"</note> of rules, nor adore
Your *tyrant-text*; but by this dawning light
Which streams upon me through your *three-layered night* <note>original: "three-pil'd night"; "three-piled" refers to the thickest, highest quality velvet, used here to describe a very dense or deep ignorance</note>,
I will pass to the *East of truth*, until I may see
Man’s first, fair state; when wise *simplicity*,

The *Dove* and *Serpent*, *innocent* and *wise*,
Dwell in his breast, and he in *Paradise*.
There, from the tree of knowledge's best boughs,
I will pluck a garland for *Eugenius’s* brows,
Which fame shall bequeath to succeeding times
With this most just applause: Great *Vaughan's* wreath!

## On the Author's Vindication and
## Reply to the Blasphemy, and *En-*
## *thusiastic Frenzy* of the
## *Distracted Alazonomastix.* <term>Alazonomastix: A pseudonym used by the philosopher Henry More, meaning "scourge of boasters."</term>

Hail great *Eugenius!* look what force you had
To wind and *turn* your *adversary* mad.
By my faith! *I will petition* for him: will you be
But merciful, and cure his *lethargy* <note>original: "Lethargie"; here used to mean a state of mental stupor or derangement</note>?
He has forgotten his *name*, and tells us to call
Him the *Great Sultan* <note>original: "grand Signor"; a title used for the Ottoman Sultan</note>, *Emperor* of all.
O fine *King Harry* <note>referring to Henry More</note>! who with *frantic blasts*
Overcasts both *God* and *Nature* in one *breath*.
*Arius, Sabellius* <term>Arius and Sabellius: Early Christian theologians whose views were later declared heretical</term>, all who ever wrote
Fall *short* of you for a *blasphemous wit*.
Your breath disperses *clouds*, and the vast *ocean* <note>original: "Maine"</note>
Is *checked* and *stilled* again by that same breath.
If you can *curb* the sea in *its* proud *foam*,
Why did you not *stop* the *recent invasion*? (Others
On *Yarmouth* Roads? And done something for [those]
*Of Amsterdam*, and saved your *Holy Brothers* <note>A sarcastic reference to More's fellow Puritans or Dutch religious dissenters</note>?
*Madness* alone makes you a wondrous *creature* <note>original: "Elf"</note>,
Your *miracles* work not *beyond* your *self*.

<meta>This page continues the satirical poem attacking Henry More (writing as Alazonomastix). The speaker argues that More's attempt to criticize Thomas Vaughan (Eugenius) has only succeeded in proving More’s own foolishness and Vaughan’s resilience.</meta>

When rare *Eugenius* <note>The pseudonym of Thomas Vaughan (1621–1666), an alchemist and philosopher.</note> routed your *first lines*,
He added to his *Truth*, and your *black Crimes*
*Basilico* <note>original: "Basilico"; likely referring to a "basilicon" (a royal ointment or drawing salve) used here metaphorically as a stinging medicinal retort.</note>: *Italian powder* strong,
Which drove you into *Fury* all along.
You taking the *scent* from there, it quickly bred
A *Nest* of *Scorpions* in your putrid *Head*.
Your good *Philosopher*, and *Poet* rare,
Whose learning lies not in his *Head*, but *Hair*,
Will *Cleveland's* <note>John Cleveland (1613–1658), a popular Royalist poet known for his difficult and witty style, which the author implies More is poorly imitating.</note> fancies ever scan and rhyme,
Bold *Beggar* he, to come the *Second time*!
Look to your *Ballads* both, review and scan
Your *Cantings* <term>Cantings: The use of jargon, hypocritical talk, or the specialized language of beggars and thieves.</term>, your gray *Goose* is not a *Swan*.
Indeed I never knew that noise of *words*
Used by us mortals, which your *Book* affords,
Such loud obstreperous *Cadences*, and knocks
As if your *tongue* (like *Nilus* <note>original: "Nilus"; the River Nile, specifically referring to the famous, loud cataracts of the Nile.</note>) ran on rocks.
Desist, fond thing! now we have known your *Art*,
All you have acted, is the *madman's* part.
But what have you got by this? the *world* does see,
*Eugenius'* learning, and your foolery.
The weights you put upon him, do at best
Speak of him as a *Palm* <note>The palm tree was a traditional symbol of resilience because it was believed to grow stronger when weighted down (Palma crescit sub pondere).</note>, he cannot be oppressed;
For against *virtue* there is no *Success*,
You make him *more*, when you would make him less.

P. B. Master of Arts, of Oxford. <note>original: "Oxoniensis"</note>

# THE SECOND WASH.

It is the advice of *Solomon*, and he is a significant figure <note>original: "some-body"</note> in my library: *Answer a fool according to his folly, lest he seem wise in his own conceit* <note>Proverbs 26:5. This biblical justification was a common defense for using satire and insults in religious and philosophical debates.</note>. It may be that the *Proverbs* are not in the *Canon* <term>Canon: The official list of books considered to be authoritative scripture.</term> of *Granta* <note>Granta: A poetic or historical name for Cambridge University, where Henry More was a fellow.</note>, since it is in her interest to tolerate fools for the sake of her children <note>Vaughan implies that Cambridge University protects its own "foolish" scholars at the expense of biblical wisdom.</note>. However, I believe my author is orthodox <term>Orthodox: Following correctly established religious doctrines.</term>, and I build my freedom upon his precept. He is a good Christian who acts according to the *text*, and because I...

### The Second Wash.

...will not transgress; thus I obey.

Stand back, you offspring of Ham <note>original: "sprats of Cham." Ham (Cham) was the son of Noah, traditionally associated with a curse or lack of refinement. Vaughan uses "sprats" to call them small, insignificant fish.</note>, you double-faced small fry, the fish and flesh of the University <note>original: "Versity." Vaughan uses this shorthand to mock Cambridge University.</note>, *Mastix* and *Philomastix*! <note>These names refer to Henry More’s pseudonyms, such as Alazonomastix (the "Scourge of Braggarts") and his supporters.</note>

->——— I am here before you, whom you seek. ———<-
<note>original: "Coram quem quaeritis, adsum." This is a quote from Virgil’s Aeneid, where Aeneas reveals himself to Dido. Vaughan uses it to announce his entry into the debate.</note>

Make room for your betters, you shrimps! You shoal of empty shells! I am ready for you, *Mastix*, once more—and may the wise forgive me for this dance upon a cracked skull! <note>original: "footing of a crackt scull." Vaughan mockingly apologizes for wasting his time arguing with a "cracked" or insane opponent.</note>

But *Knipperdoling* <note>Bernhard Knipperdolling (c. 1495–1536) was a leader of the radical Anabaptist rebellion in Münster. Vaughan uses the name as an insult to imply More is a religious fanatic.</note> is barricaded; there is a *Block* <note>A pun on "blockhead" or a physical obstruction.</note> between us which I must kick out of my way. It is *Johannes Philomastix*, a new *John of Leyden* <note>John of Leyden (1509–1536) was the self-proclaimed "King" of the Münster Rebellion; another reference to radical fanaticism.</note>, and a peer to brother *Knip*. Is it that "ingenious" young man *J. T.*, or is it not *Jack Taylor* the *Water-Poet*? <note>John Taylor (1578–1653) was a popular poet and Thames waterman. Scholars like Vaughan often looked down on him as an uneducated "low" writer.</note>

Alas, poor *John*! I should sentence you to a meager *Lent* <term>Lent: A period of fasting and penitence.</term>, but your Verse has so much *Famine* in it that it cannot even pass for a fast from wit. <note>Vaughan implies Taylor's poetry is so naturally empty of intelligence that he cannot claim it is a deliberate "fast."</note> You truly have very strange qualities: you can sting with your teeth. Go on your way as a *Jack Straw* <note>Jack Straw was a leader of the Peasants' Revolt of 1381. The name implies a man of no substance or a low-born rebel.</note>; you have no...

# The Second Wash.

...substance enough for **Jack Block**, I have stepped over you already.

And now, my dirty *Marrano* <note>original: "Marrano." Historically a derogatory term for Sephardic Jews in Spain who were forced to convert to Christianity; Vaughan uses it here as a general insult for a hypocrite or an outcast.</note>, I can see a scarecrow, a rhyming bogeyman <note>original: "Bug." In the 17th century, a "bug" or "bugbear" referred to a ghost or imaginary monster used to frighten children.</note> named and called <note>original: "hight and ycleped." Both are archaic Middle English terms for "named."</note> **Moore**. He is, reader, a mere shadow, a mask <note>original: "Mascara." From the Italian *maschera*, meaning a mask or a puppet.</note>, a thing so far removed from learning that he is hardly even a metaphor for a scholar. He has, indeed, a certain provincialism <note>original: "Patavinitie." A term used by ancient Roman critics to describe the historian Livy's regional style from Padua; Vaughan uses it to mock More’s lack of refined wit.</note>, some crude allusions like jumbled verses <note>original: "Macaronics." A style of poetry that mixes Latin words with the common language, often for comic or nonsensical effect.</note> to Latin, but if we judge his discourse accurately, it is a complete body of grammatical errors <note>original: "solæcisms." A solecism is a grammatical mistake or a breach of social etiquette.</note>. To reply to his "Frolics," as he calls them, would be to bray at an ass, like the aldermen in *Cervantes* <note>Vaughan refers to a story in *Don Quixote* where two city officials search for a lost donkey by imitating its braying so perfectly that they end up braying at each other in the dark.</note>. It is a degree of madness to imitate one who is already insane, and I do not love a madman <note>original: "Bedlam." A reference to London’s Bethlem Royal Hospital for the mentally ill.</note> so much that I would become one just for company.

It is my intention to justify the **Truth**, not myself, for the quarrel is not personal; it is the interest of **Nature** more than **mine**. Where he pretends to reason, there I will reveal his distraction <note>Vaughan uses "distraction" here in the sense of "distraughtness" or mental instability.</note>. I will disprove his "sobriety"—

# The Second Wash.

...as so many *remissions* of his *fit* <note>original: "Remissions of the Fit." Periods during a fever or a bout of madness where the symptoms temporarily lessen.</note>, and these also proceed from my correction, for he is chained in by my *principles*. If you catch him in his own *liberty*, he *acts like a madman* <note>original: "madds it."</note> all over; you shall only observe that he *ebbs* and *flows*, continuing in the same *frenzy*, but in several *degrees*.

The first thing I meet with is a *letter* <note>original: "Pistle," a shortened form of "Epistle."</note> to his *student*, and I scorn to even look at it. It is a *conspiracy* like that of *tricksters* <note>original: "Juglers." In the 17th century, this often referred to charlatans or deceivers rather than circus performers.</note>, for he dared not address his *scribblings* to anyone but someone who *submitted* <note>original: "truckl'd under him." To truckle meant to sleep in a trundle bed under a master's bed, implying a submissive or fawning relationship.</note> to him. His next *lamentation* is to the *reader*, and here this "divine universalized" *scrap of a man* <note>original: "Knip." Likely a derogatory term for someone small or insignificant, like a "snippet."</note> charges me with *unchristian bitterness*. To this *whine* I shall answer with a *truth*: that I was more *just* than *bitter*. The language I bestowed on him in that *reply* was appropriate <note>original: "apposit."</note> and suited him perfectly: I did but hold the *mirror* <note>original: "Glasse."</note> for him, and now having seen his own scurvy face, he condemns his own *appearance* <note>original: "Physiognomy." The practice of judging character or temperament from facial features.</note>.

# The Second Wash.

->B 3<-

I had two significant motives for that performance: *provocation* and *reason*. Both of these were missing from his first *libel* <note>original: "Libel." In this period, a libel referred to a published statement—often a pamphlet—that was considered defamatory or insulting.</note>. All the world knows that I have been provoked, and most virulently too, without having given any offense myself. That I had *reason* is apparent, for I spoke no more than what I knew, and the *truth* is harmful to no man. I did not put pen to paper without a certain, express knowledge of my *adversary*—and this in his own handwriting—for I have portrayed him in my own *discourse* exactly as I found him in his.

It will perhaps be objected that I have called him an *owl*, a *mouse*, and a *Moor* <note>original: "Moore." A pun on the name of his opponent, the philosopher Henry More.</note>. These are terms that do not align with the zeal of certain overly strict, "cropped" professors <note>original: "cropt Professors." A derogatory term for Puritans or "Roundheads" who wore their hair short and were known for their intense, often judgmental, religious profession of faith.</note>. *Truly, gentlemen*, you are being oversensitive <note>original: "scrupulous." Here meaning excessively concerned with minor points of morality or etiquette.</note>; these are not such *thorns* that even a very tender conscience cannot rest upon them without being pricked. If it is a fault to call him...

<summary>Thomas Vaughan justifies his harsh language against Henry More by claiming he was provoked and only described More as he truly is. He mocks critics who find his insults unchristian or overly aggressive.</summary>
<keywords>Thomas Vaughan, Henry More, polemic, libel, provocation, cropped professors, puns, 17th-century satire</keywords>

...a mouse—what a transgressor is he who made himself so? Why did he *frisk* about and nibble at the margins of my book? Why did he *vomit* on my innocent and *snowy* pages? Or, to speak in my first *allusion*, leave his mouse-droppings <note>original: *μυοχόδον* (*myochodon*)</note> behind him and then *run into his hole*? I must confess I had the use of this *notion* from a *Christian*, but the reason from a *Moor* <note>Vaughan is punning on the name of his adversary, the philosopher Henry More.</note>. 

The glorious and reverend *Spalato* <note>Marco Antonio de Dominis (1560–1624), the Archbishop of Spalato, who was a famous religious controversialist of the era.</note> is my precedent. For this learned father, to wash off the dirt of *Leonardus Marius*, published a sharp *reply* with this appropriate title: *The First Shrew* <note>original: *Sorex primus*. In Latin, *sorex* can refer to a shrew-mouse or a field mouse.</note>. It is the nature of that *vermin* to gnaw the outside of things; and since the efforts of this *Cambridge scribbler* were nothing but so many *marginal scratches*, I could find nothing like him except *a mouse*. 

But there is one more fault in my *stock* <note>original: "stock," referring here to his inventory of insults or his collection of arguments.</note>: I have called him a *Moor*, and how shall I *wash* that away? I believe I need not tell you it

is his name, and if you take it in my sense, it is his *nature*, for he is nothing *candid* <note>original: "candid." Vaughan is punning on the Latin *candidus*, which means "white" or "shining," as well as "sincere." He is saying More is neither white-skinned nor honest.</note>. As for the *owl* I tell him of, had he been hatched under a *hen*, he could have been no other *bird*, for he has much of the *species* <note>In this context, his essential nature or kind.</note> in him. He avoids the light so perfectly, as if he had newly shed his feathers and were a *man* only by *metempsychosis* <note>The philosophical concept of the transmigration of souls, where a soul passes at death into a new body, even of a different species.</note>. 

But to give you a more serious account of these *terms*, it is the practice of Christ himself—and that, I hope, is not un-Christian—to *characterize men* by those things which they resemble. Thus has he called *Herod* a *fox*, and elsewhere he styles *Judas* a *devil*; false pastors he calls *wolves*, the buyers and sellers *thieves*, and those *Hebrew puritans*, the *Pharisees*, *hypocrites* <note>Vaughan uses "Hebrew puritans" to compare the biblical Pharisees to the rigid religious faction of his own day.</note>. For when he reproves the *Cretians* <note>The people of Crete; St. Paul famously quoted a Cretan poet saying, "Cretans are always liars, evil beasts, lazy gluttons" (Titus 1:12).</note>, he makes use of that ig-

<header>The Second Wash.</header>

ignominious proverb, *Evil beasts and lazy gluttons* <note>original: "slow bellies"; a reference to Titus 1:12.</note>. When the *High Priest* commanded the *Jews* to *strike* him on the face, he replied to him, not without some bitterness, *God shall strike you, you whitewashed wall* <note>A reference to Acts 23:3, where St. Paul rebukes the High Priest Ananias for hypocrisy.</note>. I do not cite these passages to justify a *harmful malice* <note>original: "injurious spleen"; the spleen was traditionally considered the source of anger or spite.</note>, but to argue for the *freedom* of the *Truth*. For *Christ* and his *Apostle* did not apply these terms to the *just* but to the *unjust*; likewise, I did not rebuke my *adversary* until I found him to be exactly as I described him.

But he has *abused a man unknown* to him, and one who never offended him in the slightest imaginable way; this is the *truth, Reader*, and nothing else. But this *Knip* <note>Possibly a derogatory nickname or reference to an insignificant person.</note>, this distracted *mulatto* <note>A racial slur used here as a pun on Henry More's last name, "More," to imply he is "dark-natured" or "impure."</note> complains because I tell him of his *faults*, and assumes for himself a *Royal Prerogative* to cause offense without receiving *correction*. Vaughan is wittily claiming the status to judge or "appoint" More's character.</note>.

# The Second Wash.

*The Faerie Queene* <note>Henry More was an admirer of Edmund Spenser and wrote poetry in a similar style; Vaughan suggests that More's literary pretensions will not protect him.</note> shall not save you.

Although I know it is an *action* beneath my *self* to answer this ridiculous *scribbler*—it being somewhat of a *lapse in judgment* <note>original: "Desertion of sobrietie"</note> to prosecute a *madman* <note>original: "Bedlam"</note>—I will nevertheless do him this *honor* for the sake of the *truth*. I am not concerned with whom I oppose, but with what I defend. The *supernatural whims* <note>original: "firks"; here meaning sudden impulses or quirks of the mind.</note> and *frenzies* <note>original: "Raptures"</note> in his *book* are clear evidence of his *insanity* <note>original: "Distraction"</note>. I have no doubt that the *boys* in the street will hoot at him once they become familiar with his *tricks*. He is truly mad in the *highest degree*, and he lacks nothing for his *graduation* <note>original: "Commencement"; a pun on the university graduation ceremony, suggesting his madness is his only qualification.</note> except a *ballad* to celebrate the occasion. I have no way to protect my own *reputation* <note>original: "Credit"</note> except to *pick out* <note>original: "cull"</note> the moments when he is *lucid* <note>original: "Intermissions"; referring to the intervals of sanity in a madman.</note> and address him when his *fit* least affects him. This is the path I intend to take. And *readers*—those of you who *know me*—do not think that I am mad simply because I deal with someone who is; I do it for his *cure*, not to *imitate* him.

# The Second Wash.

You are welcome, **More** <note>Henry More (1614–1687), the Cambridge Platonist and Vaughan’s intellectual rival.</note>, to this **narrow region**, and truly you **speak like a giant—a porter of some enchanted castle**—who would rout a **knight-errant** with **syllables**. For my part, I have neither **Morglay** <note>The legendary sword of Sir Bevis of Hampton.</note> nor **Arundel** <note>The legendary horse of Sir Bevis, though here used to imply knightly equipment or support.</note>, but I will make you wish yourself in the **clouds** again, or that you had brought **thunder** in your hands to **support** you. It will perhaps be said that this is **desperate**, but Reader—if I may speak to you (for perhaps you will not hear me now that I am in danger)—we are likely to have a **fair game** of it. This **tall thing** is already shrunk to a **mouse**, and he who made such a **mark on the earth** <note>original: "spot of the Earth"</note> will be committed to a **trap**. Strange **alterations** indeed! But be confident of **such miracles**, gentlemen. They are not altogether **impossible**; just read on, and I will give you **evidence**.

-># The Second Wash.<-

*Imitation*. From the first page of his book (which by his calculation is the third) to the very 57th, he is in a medley of infirmities. Sometimes he tries to excuse his *incivilities* <note>original: "Incivilities"; rudeness or lack of manners.</note> toward me with a faint *extenuation* <note>original: "Extenuation"; an attempt to make an offense seem less serious.</note> of his first spite; but while he blots out the old *libel*, he composes and scribbles a *new* one. Sometimes he forgets me again, and he does not only leave *me* behind, but poor humanity in general. He is hoisted above us with a sudden *metaphysical pulley* and snatched up into a wild *universalization of spirit*. At this *height*, I thought I had lost him, for I never expected his *return*. But my *Stentor* <note>In Greek mythology, Stentor was a herald with a voice as loud as fifty men; here, Vaughan uses it to mock More's loud and boastful tone.</note> opens his mouth unexpectedly; nor does he speak from *Earth*, gentlemen, but from *Heaven*, and in a style like that of an *Ottoman* <note>original: "Ottoman"; referring to the Ottoman Sultan, implying a tyrannical, grandiose, or arrogant tone.</note> emperor.

I say the force and authority <note>original: "warrant"</note> of both *Nouns* and *Verbs* comes from their *use*, etc. *Oyez*! <note>original: "O yez!"; a traditional call for silence and attention in a court of law.</note> *Master Bust of Eton* <note>Richard Bust (1548–1613), a schoolmaster at Eton College, where Henry More was educated. such a word, beyond your scribblings, is intended to cover up, not to reveal <note>original: "discover"</note>. It is plain, then, that the use of *Nouns* and *Verbs* depends on their inherent meaning <note>original: "force"; the natural power or expressive energy of a word</note>, and not their *meaning* on their *use*. This is a *Truth* so clear and *known* even to children, that no one but a *Master* of *Arts* from *Cambridge* could be ignorant of it.

But you have another *tool* to prove me no *Orator*. In your opinion, a person who is an *Orator* must speak, but *Eugenius Philalethes* only wrote. From this, it would follow that an *Oration* is *not an oration* if it is written. It seems, then, that *Cicero's* <note>Marcus Tullius Cicero (106–43 BCE), the Roman statesman widely considered one of history's greatest orators.</note> orations are *not orations* now; they were only so at certain times, when they were delivered at the *Rostra* <note>The public platform in the Roman Forum from which speakers addressed the citizens.</note>.

Truly, my friend, if the *ears* <note>original: "Flaps"</note> of *Midas* <note>In Greek mythology, King Midas was given the ears of a donkey by Apollo as a punishment for his foolish musical judgment.</note> were stitched to your *head* <note>original: "Block"</note>, they would thrive as if by *transplantation*. They would be much more like an *ass's ears* on you than they ever were *formerly* on him.

<meta>This page continues Thomas Vaughan’s (writing as Eugenius Philalethes) aggressive rebuttal to Henry More (Master Mastix). Vaughan is currently critiquing More’s reliance on Aristotle’s definition of the soul, arguing that Aristotle defines the soul by its functions rather than its actual substance.</meta>

<page-num>19</page-num>
<header>The Second Wash.</header>

the soul from *essential faculties*; and therefore his definition is *essential*. A juggling, halting conclusion <note>original: "Consequence"; in logic, the conclusion that follows from premises.</note>. The question is, *what is it?* <note>original: τί ἐστίν (ti estin)</note>, *What is the soul?* <note>original: Quid sit Anima</note>. I ask not what the soul does, but what the soul *is*? 

But *Aristotle*, though he has proposed and prescribed the question *What is it?* as the main *fundamental* of *demonstrations* <note>Aristotle argued that to demonstrate something, one must first define its essence.</note>, answers not at all to it in his definition. <page-num>31</page-num>

To this I answer, that by *rarefaction* you either understand an absolute *conversion* of some parts of the *blood* into *vapors*, or else a *vital refining* <note>original: "vitall subtileation," meaning the process of making a substance more refined, spiritualized, or thin</note>, by which the blood is hindered from thickening and corrupting; and this is nothing else but a *commotion* or *stirring* of the parts. 

That there is a *commotion* of parts in the *sea* is evident—for what else is the *tide*? That there is also a *rarefaction* of some parts into *vapors* is a *thing* known to all the world. This happens not only in the *element of water*, but in the *earth* itself, which corresponds to the *flesh* of an animal. The *central sun* or *spirit* does not only cause a *tide*, but with its *heat* it sublimes <note>original: "sublimes," an alchemical term for purifying a substance or turning a solid/liquid into vapor through heat</note> the *moisture* into *clouds*, which *evaporate* <note>original: "transpire"</note> at the *pores* of the

Earth. And thus, *Mastix* <note>The pseudonym Vaughan uses for his rival, Henry More</note>, the *spirit* has a double action, comparable to the functions of *life* in *animals*. First of all, it has a *pulse* in the *sea*, just as the *soul* has in the *blood*. Secondly, its *heat* converts some parts of the *water* into *vapors*, which escape at the *surface* <note>original: "superficies"</note> of the *earth*; and this *action* corresponds to that of our *animal heat*, which expels *excess moisture* at the *pores*. This *refined* <note>original: "subtill"</note> *evaporation* we commonly call *sweating*.

I have now, you rascal <note>original: "sirrah," a term used to address someone of lower status or to express contempt</note>, refuted your *tool* of *rarefaction* <note>the process of a substance thinning or expanding, which More argued did not happen in the sea</note>. The next point is your *universal diffusion* of the *stroke at once*. This, I must confess, is an obscure expression, but elsewhere you explain it: *Your meaning is that the tide* <note>original: "flux and reflux"</note> *is not in all places at once.* 

You are indeed an *owl in cuerpo* <note>original: "in cuerpo," a Spanish phrase meaning "in the body"—that is, without a cloak, or essentially naked/exposed</note>, a *thing unfledged*, and not even *pin-feathered* <note>meaning a young bird that has not yet grown mature feathers</note>. Are you not ashamed to show your bare *rump*? You do not have so much as a *fig leaf* to cover it.

<summary>Vaughan argues that the Earth "sweats" through evaporation just like a living animal and mocks Henry More as an "unfledged owl" for his failure to understand the mechanics of the tides.</summary>

# The Second Wash

...fig leaf to hide your *ignorance* and *deformity*. Every critic of motion <note>original: "Motion Mastix"</note> (and such is this *tide* <note>original: "flux"</note>) has two distinct endpoints: the point *from which* and the point *to which* <note>original: "*à Quo* and *ad Quem*"</note>. Every *medium* through which a *motion* must occur has—as the *philosophers* put it—*parts distinct from other parts* <note>original: "*partes extra partes*," a philosophical term referring to the extension of matter in space</note>. The *moving force* <note>original: "movent"</note> is then required to move from part to part—that is to say, sequentially—because motion is a sequential action. 

How, then, is it possible for the pulse to be in *all* parts or places at once, unless you are dreaming of a *metaphysical motion that is everywhere at once* <note>original: "Ubiquitary Metaphysical motion"</note>, which cannot be found in nature? Go your way to *Master Bust* of *Eton* <note>Henry Bust was a famously strict headmaster at Eton College; Vaughan is suggesting More needs a schoolboy's whipping for his poor logic</note>, not just for a *second lash*, but for a hundred and fifty lashes. 

Thus you see, Sir, what pitiful objections you have made; yet, despite this, you have built your *victory* and *triumph* upon these very *cracks* in your *skull*. You tell me that I will never make sense of the *tide* <note>original: "Flux and reflux"</note> until I am able...

But now, my friend, I will take your *sponge* in hand and squeeze it for a specific purpose.

purpose. I say then that *rarefaction* and *condensation* do not happen *in the manner of a sponge* <note>original: "per modum Spongiæ"</note>. For a *sponge*, whether stretched or contracted, still keeps the form of a *sponge*. However, *water* thinned into *vapors* no longer has the *nature* <term>original: "Complexion"; in this context, the fundamental constitution or temperament of a substance</term> of *water*; and on the contrary, *vapors* condensed into *water* no longer retain the *nature* of *vapors*. 

A *sponge* is stretched and contracted solely by *filling* <term>original: "Impletion"</term> and *emptying* <term>original: "Evacuation"</term>—that is to say, without any change to its physical parts. But *water* is thinned, and *vapor* is thickened, by a different kind of action: namely, by an absolute change of the *substance* <note>original: "Body"</note>. 

It is clear then that both of these *modes* <note>methods or ways of being</note> are truly distinct and different. Therefore, to say that *rarefaction* and *condensation* happen *in the manner of a sponge* is nonsense in a proper *university*, but passes for *philosophy* in *Cambridge* <note>Henry More was a fellow at Christ's College, Cambridge. Vaughan, who studied at Oxford, is insulting the quality of logic taught at his rival university.</note>. Thus, Sir, I have returned your *sponge*, and a well-squeezed one at that. Go now to your

-># The Second Wash.<-

By the pulse of the *great world* <term>great world: the macrocosm, or the universe viewed as a giant living organism.</term>, we may easily know why some portion of *seawater*, being separated from the whole, is not subject to a *tide* <note>original: "Flux"</note> as it was formerly. We see that the *blood*, as long as it is in the *veins*, is *animated* <note>original: "actuated"</note> by the *pulse*; but if any part is let out, that part is no longer *animated*, for it is beyond the *sphere of activity*, and the pulse cannot reach it. 

It is just so with *seawater*; for as long as it is in the *general channel* <note>the ocean</note>, where the *pulse* of the *great world* beats, it is within the *sphere* of the *central spirit*. But once being disconnected from the whole, it is separated from the *region* of the *pulse* and is no longer subject to a *tide*.

You say I have used *reminiscence* <note>original: "Reminiscencie"; the Platonic idea that learning is actually the soul "remembering" knowledge it possessed before being born into a body.</note> as an argument to prove the *pre-existence* of the *soul* before *her entrance into the body*. These are your words, and the reader may recognize them by that phrase "pre-existence before her entrance." But I must deal with the meaning aimed at by your *nonsense*, and I say it is false. 

I mentioned *reminiscence* to show you what the *doctrine* of *Plato* was—whom you falsely pretend to understand—and as for pre-existence, I have far better arguments to prove it. However, that is needless, for you yourself grant it. It is my task to prove that the *soul* has an explicit, methodical knowledge before her entrance into the body, and for this purpose, *reminiscence* is relevant. But you are *mad*, and you speak without knowing what you are saying. I say then, and positively affirm, that the *soul*, before her *immersion* in matter, is a knowing, intelligent spirit—and this *actually* <note>In philosophy, "actually" means the soul possesses fully realized knowledge, rather than just the "potential" to learn.</note>,

and explicitly. This is my *doctrine*, and this is how I prove it. If the soul is not an *intelligent substance* before its *entrance* into the body, then that *faculty* by which *Aristotle* defines the soul—namely intelligence—is not an *essential faculty*, but something *acquired* later. But *More* <note>Henry More (1614–1687), the Cambridge Platonist whom Vaughan is attacking.</note> has previously granted that it is an *essential faculty*. *Therefore* <note>original: "Ergo"</note>, etc. The *major premise* <note>original: "major"; the first part of a logical syllogism.</note> is invincible, for whatever is *essential* to the soul can never be *separated* from it without the *destruction* of its *essence*. So, if *intelligence* is an *essential* faculty, the soul must necessarily be *intelligent* both *before* and *after* its *incorporation* <note>The process of the soul entering a physical, "corporeal" body.</note>.

Now, as for its explicit, *actual* knowledge in the state of *pre-existence* <note>original: "Præexistencie"</note>, I will prove it *with even stronger reason* <note>original: "à fortiori"; a logical term suggesting that if a lesser truth is certain, then a greater one must be even more so.</note>. the matter. But she can actually understand while in the body; therefore, she can do so much more when she is free from the body.

Again, if the soul has an intelligent, essential faculty in the state of *pre-existence* <note>original: "Præexiſtencie"; the state of the soul existing before the birth of the body.</note>, she must also actually understand in that same state; and I demonstrate the logical consequence as follows: Either she actually understands, or her faculties are suppressed and cast asleep. But her faculties are not suppressed and cast asleep; *therefore* <note>original: "Ergo"</note>. This division is immediate <note>meaning there is no third possibility; it is either one or the other</note>, for one of the two must necessarily be true. And I prove the *minor premise* <note>the second part of the logical argument: "her faculties are not suppressed"</note> thus: 

If the soul is cast asleep before her entrance into the body, she might also be cast asleep after her separation from the body, for she returns then to her former state. If this were true, an heretical *soul-sleep* <note>original: "Pſychopannuchie"; from the Greek *psychopannychia*, the belief that the soul "sleeps" and is unconscious between death and the resurrection.</note> would follow—a view which *More* <note>Henry More (1614–1687), the Cambridge Platonist philosopher whom Vaughan is debating.</note> opposed in his poems.

Again, if the faculties of the soul are suppressed in her *pre-existence*, this

<summary>Vaughan argues that if the soul is consciously intelligent while trapped in a physical body, it must be even more so before birth. He warns that suggesting the soul is "asleep" before birth would logically mean it also "sleeps" after death, a heresy known as soul-sleep.</summary>

# The Second Wash.

Begone, you ignorant, stupid scribbler! How dare you pretend to understand philosophy when you cannot even understand common English?

Your next charge, Harry, is so false that I would think you were a committee-man <note>original: "Committe-man"; a member of a local administrative committee during the English Civil War, often criticized for corruption or overreach.</note>, except that you have more to do with Bedlam <note>original: "Bedlam"; a common name for the Bethlem Royal Hospital for the insane.</note> than with the Commonwealth <note>original: "Common-wealth"; the republican government of England at the time.</note>. You say that I admit to two of Aristotle's principles: matter and privation. I told you previously that matter was a principle of nature, and I found it in the "book of nature," not in Aristotle. Privation, indeed, is a creation of the Schools <note>original: "Schooles"; referring to Medieval Scholastic philosophy, which was based heavily on Aristotle.</note>—a Greek fable, but no true principle. This is because principles, properly speaking, are the substantial parts or ingredients of a compound, and privation is not such a thing. 

But the Moor <note>original: "Moor"; a pun on Henry More's surname.</note> argues: "A violet," he says, "comes from a non-violet" <note>original: "viola est ex non viola"</note>; generation <note>original: "Generation"; the process of coming into being or creation.</note> is not performed without privation <note>In Aristotelian philosophy, "privation" is the absence of a quality that is later acquired; for a thing to become something new, it must first lack that quality.</note>, and therefore privation is a principle. Well done, you faithful Peripatetic <note>original: "Peripatetick"; a follower of Aristotle's philosophy.</note>! Now at last I begin to understand you: privation...

# The Second Wash.

...committed no absurdities. The first thing you object to <note>original: "except at"; to take exception to or protest against.</note> is a task of mine: that I should spend a whole spring to find out this conclusion: *That things that are produced in nature are out of something in nature unlike the things produced.* To prove this, you make use of my own words, which are these: *I took to task the fruits of one spring*, and then follows this learned comment. I say (says *Henry More*) that one *spring* may signify a whole *spring*, and your making a "task" of it seems to determine the words to that sense. 

Oh rare, metaphysical nonsense! Readers, I charge you to take notice of it. The fruits indeed were the fruits of one *spring* <note>Vaughan is clarifying that he studied the literal produce of a single springtime season, not that he spent the entire duration of the season performing a single experiment.</note>, but that I took them to task for *one whole spring* is a meaning nowhere to be found. If this holds true, there are more *principles* than *three*, and *Aristotle* has fallen short of their *number*. First of all, the *Sun* contributes to *generation*; or according to *Aristotle* himself, "*Sun and man beget man*" <note>original: "Sol et Homo generant Hominem"; a famous Aristotelian maxim suggesting that biological life depends on both a physical parent and the vitalizing heat of the sun.</note>.
