Secondly, the *parents* do *contribute*, and are necessary *agents* in all *univocal generations* <note>original: "Univocall Generations"; reproduction where the offspring is of the same species as the parent (e.g., humans producing humans).</note>; therefore, the *parents* are principles. Thirdly, no *generation* can possibly be performed without *time* and *place*, any more than without *privation* <note>original: "privation"; in Aristotelian physics, the absence of a form or quality that must exist before a change can occur.</note>, and therefore *time* and *place* are *principles*. See now, my friend, the *consequences* of your *nonsense*. I must therefore tell you that a principle is a *constitutive part* <note>A part that physically makes up or "constitutes" the substance of a body.</note> of the *body*, but *privation* is *destructive*, as we see in *men*. # The Second Wash.

The **first matter** <note>original: "first Matter"; in alchemy, the *prima materia* is the fundamental, formless substance from which all things are created. Vaughan claims to have physically interacted with it.</note> I speak of is the first matter of all things, and I have seen it and felt it ten thousand times. > The **"first matter"** (you say) **"is a substance out of which all physical things are made, but is itself made out of nothing."**

This is your positive assertion, and you follow it with a **question** <note>original: "Quere"; a Latin form of "query."</note>: whether that **matter**, which I say I have seen and handled, is the kind that will fit with this **"Idea"**? 

It does, sir, for this **"Idea"** fits with the **matter**. It is that **seed** <note>original: "sperm"; here used in the archaic sense of a biological or cosmic "generative principle" or "source-seed."</note> which God created out of nothing in the beginning of His ways, and out of which He made **Heaven** and **Earth** and all that are within them.

"So, it seems then you had a desire to write poetical prose," and so on. He enters with his *stanza* in his mouth and a *blunder* <note>original: "Bull," a term for a ridiculous logical error or a self-contradictory statement.</note> to keep it company. *"Let us," (he says) "bring it all forth entirely into view."*

But let us look at the text itself. "For I call matter" <note>original Greek: *Λέγω γὰρ ὕλην* (Legō gar hylēn)</note>, etc. "For I say," he says, "that matter is the primary subject of every thing, from which—being inherent in it—something arises, and not by accident; and into which, if anything is destroyed, it finally returns" <note>original Latin: *Dico enim materiam, quod rei cujusque subjectum est primum, è quo insito, non ex accidenti aliquid oritur, et in quod, si quidpiam interit, ultimum abeat.* This is a translation of Aristotle’s *Physics*, Book 1, Chapter 9.</note>. Here he is not speaking of a mere *potentiality*—and such is his *first matter*—but he speaks of a subject actually existing, as being a part of the thing generated: "from which, as an inherent part" <note>original Greek: *ἐξ οὗ ἐνυπάρχοντος* (ex hou enyparchontos)</note>, "from which, being inherent, something arises" <note>original Latin: *è quo insito aliquid oritur*</note>. Followers of Aristotle's philosophy, named after the covered walkways (peripatoi) of the Lyceum where they met.</note>. 

They have a twofold definition of matter: One that is real and qualified by their **prior dispositions** <note>original Latin: *praeviae dispositiones*. In Aristotelian physics, these are the qualities that prepare matter to receive a specific form.</note>, and this is what **Aristotle** defines in the preceding text. The other is empty and imaginary, for it does not exist in nature **absolutely** <note>original Greek: *ἁπλῶς* (haplōs)</note>, nor is it **that which is in a certain respect** <note>original Greek: *τὸ κατὰ τι* (to kata ti). This is a technical philosophical term meaning something that exists only in relation to something else, or in a specific context.</note>.

You say that my phrase **"that which is in a certain respect"** <note>original Greek: *τὸ κατὰ τι* (to kata ti)</note> is a **monster** and has one *ti* too many. Now, my friend, it seems to me that I see you like one of the *Monocoli* <note>A mythical race of people with only one leg.</note>, hopping on a single leg, for I have taken away your crutch and broken your head <note>original: "pate"</note> with it. But shall I tell you why you have cited this passage? It is because *Philo Judaeus* is a *Philo-mastix* <note>A pun by Vaughan: "Philo" (the philosopher's name/Greek for "friend") combined with "Mastix" (More's nickname), implying Philo is a friend to More's foolishness.</note>; the *Jew* speaks like the *Christian*. For the first position—namely, that the *Scripture* does not speak in this place in the *common sense* <note>original: "vulgar sense"; meaning the ordinary or literal understanding of the general public.</note>—we shall appeal to the *text itself*; and if we believe *Moses*, this is his *text*:

I say the *natural, liquid* heavens which God made do not rumble; but the *solid, fantastic, Ptolemaic system* (if there were any such thing) would rumble most terribly; for there could be no motion without the orbs <note>the concentric celestial spheres of ancient astronomy</note> necessarily breaking one another to pieces. <meta>This page continues Thomas Vaughan’s (writing as Eugenius Philalethes) rebuttal to Henry More. Having mocked More's misunderstanding of his astronomical metaphors, Vaughan now addresses More's critique of his chemical and philosophical definitions of "Air" from his earlier work, *Anthroposophia Theomagica*.</meta>

<header>The Second Wash</header>

Certainly as fast as a bird in the *air*. I said in my *Anthroposophia* <note>*Anthroposophia Theomagica*, Vaughan’s first major work on mystical philosophy and alchemy</note> that the *air* was the *body of the life of our sensory spirit* <note>original Latin: *Corpus vitæ spiritus nostri sensitivi*</note>, our "animal oil," the fuel of our vital sensory fire, without which we cannot survive for a minute. 

Henry **More** in his *Observations* concludes from these words that I have given **Aristotle** <note>The ancient Greek philosopher whose physics and definition of the four elements dominated medieval and early modern thought</note> one of his two elements back again; and he proves it thus: "If our vital and animal spirits are nourished by the *air*, then the *air* is an element of our body." 

I dare boldly, and without any insult to you, affirm that you do not know what an "element" is, nor what the word signifies. If you think this is strange, I will teach you by an example you are more capable of understanding: Our soul is a principle, but nonetheless, our soul is no element. # The Second Wash.

The Schools <note>The "Schools" refers to Scholasticism, the traditional system of theology and philosophy taught in medieval European universities based on Aristotelian logic.</note> imply a contradiction, for in condensation <term>Condensation: The process of making a substance more dense.</term> and rarefaction <term>Rarefaction: The process of making a substance thinner or less dense.</term> there is no creation or loss <note>original: "Deperdition"</note> of new matter. "All matter," says he, "has impenetrable dimensions. Therefore, if the extracted heavens lay within the compass of the Mass, they occupy the same space as the Mass and did penetrate dimensions <note>To "penetrate dimensions" was a philosophical impossibility in this period; it meant two distinct physical objects occupying the exact same space at the same time.</note>." 

Ha! Ha! He! Here is a rare and a fine conclusion. If the spirit of wine lies within the compound, or the whole body of wine, then it occupies the same space as the body itself; therefore, at the *Globe Tavern* <note>A well-known tavern in 17th-century London, used here by Vaughan to make his academic argument sound ridiculous by applying it to a common pub setting.</note>, there is "penetration of dimensions."

He *measures* the *vital mysteries of nature* by the *destructive tricks of art*, and what is most *glaring* <note>original: "grosse"</note>, he mistakes *extractions* for *multiplications*. Well, go your way as the most *perfect owl* that ever was at *Athens* <note>The owl was the symbol of Athens and wisdom; Vaughan is ironically calling More a fool</note>. Your next *argument* is a pretended *excuse* <note>original: "salvo"</note>, a new way to light *lamps*, because you want to avoid the *multiplication* of *light*, and thus your *stanza* hobbles along:

> *No new substance does that act then*
> *produce,*
> *Only the oily atoms it does excite,*
> *and wake into a flame.*

<header>The Second Wash.</header>

[When] one lamp lights another, no new light is then produced; only the *oily atoms* are excited into a *flame*. To this I say that no *oily* substance can be turned into *light*; but on the contrary, the light feeds on the *oil* and consumes it. This effect proves them to be *different substances*: for if they were one and the same, the *oil* would never be spent—because nothing devours itself—and consequently, the *light* would never go out. 

Again, my friend: every *oily body* is *passive* and cannot excite itself into a *flame* but must have some *active influence* communicated to it that may alter it into *vapor*. In this *vapor*, the *light incorporates*, and this compound of light and *vapor* we call a *flame*. Now, this *active communicated influence* is infused, *multiplied light*. 

But I must take you by the head <note>original: "noddle"; a colloquial or humorous term for the head</note>, *Mastix* <note>Vaughan’s nickname for Henry More, from the Greek word for "scourge"</note>! You speak of *atoms* as if they were established facts, but trust me, you are mistaken—and so is *Descartes* <note>original: "Des Chartes"; referring to the philosopher René Descartes, whose mechanical philosophy of atoms Vaughan frequently attacked</note>.

# The Second Wash

It is true indeed, we may imagine *atoms*, or *indivisible parts*, in any *continuum* <note>original: "Continuum"; a continuous sequence or whole in which no part is distinct from adjacent parts</note> whatsoever, but to propose this imagination as the *groundwork* of *philosophy* is something that must not be tolerated. Not a word comes out but that I snap it up; and if you will not believe me, take this *evidence*. Your *swarm* of *atoms* has left your *hive*; they fly like dust about your *mustaches* <note>original: "Mustachos"</note> and now fall on my *Philistines* <note>Likely referring to More's arguments or a mock-heroic reference to Vaughan's own targets</note>. # The Second Wash

If there be any such *things* as *atoms*, then they must be either *principles* <note>original: "Principles"; the fundamental, irreducible elements from which all other things are made</note> or *compounds*: but they are neither *principles* nor *compounds*. *Therefore, and so forth* <note>original: "Ergo, &c."</note>. This logical division is direct and therefore necessary, and I will prove its parts as follows. 

If *atoms* are *compounds*, they must have some *pre-existent principles* of which they are composed, and I desire to know what *principles* those are. For they are either composed of *atoms*, or else of divisible *substances* and *non-atoms*. As for the first possibility: if *atoms* are made of *atoms*, then both the *principles* and the *compounds* are *one and the same thing*—namely, *atoms*—which is *absurd* and also *impossible*. For many *atoms* being united must necessarily make one divisible thing, or it will follow that divisible *bodies* are not made of *atoms*, which is very true. 

If you say they are made of divisible *substances* or *non-atoms*, an equal *absurdity*...

<page-num>163</page-num>

<header>The Second Wash.</header>

...alter your opinion, and tell me, "No, *Magicus*, but *I do not.*" Well then, let us see where the difficulty lies, for we will appeal to your own words. *The difficulty (you say) is how a thing as subtle as a soul should miss a vent* <note>original: "vent." In this context, an escape route, opening, or outlet.</note> *in matter as loose* <note>original: "lax."</note> *as the first rudiments of life.* This is the difficulty, *Magicus*.

I hear you, *Maurus* <note>original: "Maurus." Latin for "Moor," used as a punning nickname for Henry More (1614–1687).</note>, and I hear at the same time the most gross nonsense and contradiction that was ever uttered. First, you tell me the difficulty does not lie in the *matter* because it is loose, but you place it elsewhere. Secondly, when you would show me where else it is, you tell me it *is* in the *matter*, because it is loose; for these are your own words: *The difficulty is how a thing as subtle as a soul should miss a vent in matter as loose as the rudiments of life.*

Ha! ha! he! Here is a circle of nonsense; if a conjurer <term>conjurer: a person who summons spirits or devils through ritual magic.</term> could but bring the Devil in, he would never get out of it. The soul

No indeed, that is not his meaning; but in a matter that is loose and fluid, the soul cannot be retained. *Hocus Pocus*, and *Presto!* This would puzzle a Gypsy and her jackman <term>jackman: an assistant to a beggar or a rogue; also used for a counterfeit scholar.</term>. In the name of Friar Rush <note>Friar Rush: A legendary folklore character, a devil who disguised himself as a friar to lead monks into sin.</note>, what are you? I remember, *Mastix* <note>original: "Mastix." Greek/Latin for "whip" or "scourge," referring to Henry More’s pen name *Alazonomastix*.</note>, you have somewhere an exorcism <note>original: "Exorcismus."</note>: give me leave to apply it to this riddle, and see if it will unfold.

> *Open you, Earth! Unclose you, fast-bound ball*
> *Of smoking darkness!*

It will not do; what an obstinate pug <note>In this context, a "pug" refers to an imp, a demon, or a stubborn person.</note> is this? Is it not possible to recall those friars who did sometimes exorcise *Ignoramus* at Cambridge? <note>A reference to George Ruggle’s 1615 play *Ignoramus*, performed at Cambridge for King James I. It satirized common lawyers and featured a scene where the character Ignoramus is "exorcised."</note> Certainly his *Riota* and his *Routa* <note>original: "Riota and his Routa." These were legal Latin terms for "riot" and "rout," personified as devils in the play *Ignoramus*.</note> were tractable, supple devils compared to this constipated <note>original: "costive."</note> nonsense of the loose <note>original: "lax."</note> matter. But let us examine this matter once more. The difficulty is the soul’s missing of an opening <note>original: "vent."</note>, and this is in the matter, which seems improbable because the matter

<meta>This page continues Thomas Vaughan’s critique of Henry More’s philosophy. Vaughan is mocking More’s logic regarding how the soul (which he views as a "subtle" or refined substance) can be contained within "lax" or fluid physical matter like sperm, while being able to pass through solid objects like brass or iron.</meta>

is loose <note>original: "lax."</note> and fluid, and this is argued by the *emphasis* of your own words, regarding such a loose matter. Is this your meaning, *Mastix* <note>A reference to Henry More’s pen name, *Alazonomastix*.</note>, or do you mean this? "No, *Magicus* <note>Thomas Vaughan's own pen name.</note>," (you say,) "I do not." Well then, my friend, what do you mean? Is the difficulty in the *soul* because it is subtle <note>original: "subtil." In 17th-century natural philosophy, "subtle" described matter that was extremely thin, refined, or spirit-like, capable of penetrating other substances.</note>? Truly, this interpretation does not agree at all with your expression; but you have, *Harry* <note>Henry More.</note>, most strange forms of speech, and a knack not known at *Babel* <note>The biblical Tower of Babel, where God confounded the languages of humanity.</note>: you can confound languages without even multiplying them.

I suppose you believe that if the *soul* were imprisoned in *brass* and *iron*, which are *solid bodies*, she would easily get out, for she can pass through any body, no matter how hard and compact it is. The mystery then is how she comes to be held <note>original: "retain'd."</note> within the *sperm*, which is a fluid, weak substance, and almost as permeable <note>original: "pervious."</note> as water. You must know that every *soul* has a natural, voluntary attachment <note>original: "application."</note> to the *sperm*,

<meta>This page continues Thomas Vaughan’s (writing as "Magicus") polemic against Henry More ("Mastix"). Vaughan is explaining his alchemical-philosophical view of how the soul inhabits the body, specifically focusing on the moment of conception within the "sperm."</meta>

<page-num>166</page-num>

<header>The Second Wash.</header>

For she *takes on* <note>original: "assumes"</note> her body not by *compulsion*, but *willingly*. This *will* or natural inclination <note>original: "propension"</note> of the *soul* is accomplished and confirmed by the *hidden ethereal garments* <note>original: "ethereall vestiments"</note> enclosed in the *sperm*. These garments are near in nature <note>original: "complexion"</note> to the very *substance* of the *Soul* <note>original: "Anima"</note>, so that she easily *mingles* with *them* as *fire* does with *air*, and thus she comes to be *held* <note>original: "retain'd"</note>. 

But this *truth* is not *fully* known to anyone but the *Adepts* <note>original: "Adepti." In alchemical traditions, an Adept is someone who has attained the highest level of mastery and secret knowledge.</note>, whose *experience* tells them what a *strong union* there is between the *Soul* and her *garments*, and how difficult a task it is to *separate* her from them. Other *philosophers* do nothing but blindly talk of *sympathy* and *antipathy*, as Henry *More* does in this *observation*. In this sense, a *sanctuary of wisdom* <note>original: "Asylum sapientiæ"</note> is actually a *sanctuary of ignorance* <note>original: "Asylum ignorantiae"</note>, for you speak of that which you do not *understand*. However, so that my readers may know I speak with reason, I will show you what *sympathy* really is,

--- Page 231 ---
<meta>This page continues Thomas Vaughan’s (writing as "Magicus") defense of his alchemical theories against the criticisms of Henry More (whom he calls "Mastix"). Vaughan is explaining the concept of "sympathy"—the natural attraction between things—to justify how the soul binds to the body.</meta>

<page-num>167</page-num>

<header>The Second Walk. <note>original: "Walk" here likely refers to a section or "wash" in his polemic.</note></header>

<sig>M 4</sig>

and where it comes from. *Sympathy* is nothing more than a natural application, or an inclination <note>original: "propension"</note> that one thing has toward another by its own nature. It arises from a shared proportion <note>original: "contemperature"</note> in some measure, or a correspondence <note>original: "symbol"</note> of qualities and temperaments <note>original: "complexions"</note>. Thus, *Fire* shares part of the same *temperature* <note>In Early Modern science, "temperature" referred to the specific mixture of the four primary qualities: hot, cold, moist, and dry.</note> with *Air*, and *Air* with *Water*, so that the *inferior* element easily receives and admits the *superior*, and the *superior* just as easily unites with the *inferior*. 

Now, my friend, I will address those objections which you have imagined <note>original: "fansied"</note> in the dark against my analogy of the flame and the candle. I told you, *Mastix* <note>Vaughan's derogatory nickname for Henry More; a "Mastix" is a "scourge" or "whipper."</note>, that within every sperm <note>In this context, "sperm" refers to the generative seed or life-principle of any living thing.</note> there was a hidden *ethereal moisture* <note>a subtle, celestial fluid believed to sustain life</note>, which is similar in nature <note>original: "neer of complexion"</note> to the *fire* of the *Soul* <note>original: "Anima"</note>. Through this moisture, the *Soul* was united to the sperm, just as light is united to a candle. You call this a *poetic illustration* only because it is a *physical example* <note>original: "physicall instance"</note>. But this is certainly no news to me; I know you are speaking...

<meta>catchword: Buls (The next page begins with the word "Bulls," likely continuing a critique of More's logic or "bulls.")</meta>

<summary>Vaughan defines sympathy as a natural attraction based on shared qualities between elements and explains that the soul (Anima) binds to the body’s "sperm" via a shared "ethereal moisture." He defends his use of the candle analogy against Henry More's dismissal of it as mere poetry.</summary>
